113:["$","summary",null,{"className":"flex cursor-pointer list-none items-center justify-between gap-4 font-medium [&::-webkit-details-marker]:hidden","children":["How much memory depth does a beginner oscilloscope need?",["$","span",null,{"className":"flex h-7 w-7 shrink-0 items-center justify-center rounded-full border border-border text-accent transition-transform duration-300 group-open:rotate-45","children":"+"}]]}]
114:["$","p",null,{"className":"mt-3 text-sm text-muted leading-6","children":"Memory decides how long the scope can capture at full speed: capture time equals memory divided by sample rate. At 1 GSa/s, 24 Mpts holds 24 ms, while a 10K-point record holds just 10 µs before the scope must slow its sampling. Deep memory matters for serial packets, power-up sequences and intermittent glitches. For looking at repeating waveforms, a shallow record is less of a handicap."}]

On a mains-powered bench scope, every probe's ground clip is tied to earth, so clipping it onto a live or neutral conductor can create a short circuit, destroy the probe and injure you.
